            (prefix + &amp;quot;hop_rate_ratio_shell.png&amp;quot;, &amp;quot;rate_ratio_to_far&amp;quot;, &amp;quot;hop rate(shell) / hop rate(far)&amp;quot;, 1.0, True,
             f&amp;quot;{name}: hop rate relative to far Li (&amp;lt;1 = slower near the dopant)&amp;quot;),
            (prefix + &amp;quot;nonback_ratio_shell.png&amp;quot;, &amp;quot;nonback_ratio_to_far&amp;quot;, &amp;quot;non-back hop rate(shell) / (far)&amp;quot;, 1.0, True,
             f&amp;quot;{name}: hops that do not undo the previous hop, relative to far Li&amp;quot;)):
        if plot_hop_shell(rows, names, key, yl, os.path.join(outdir, fn), ttl, hline=hl, skip_far=sk):
            out.append(fn)
    return out

I'll write the script and test it on your CIF.&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/div&gt;&lt;span class=&quot;file-pill missing&quot;&gt;max_dopant_sites.py (not included)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/section&gt;&lt;section class=&quot;msg user&quot;&gt;&lt;h3&gt;User&lt;/h3&gt;&lt;div class=&quot;text&quot;&gt;&lt;pre&gt;dont test...present file first&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/div&gt;&lt;/section&gt;&lt;section class=&quot;msg assistant&quot;&gt;&lt;h3&gt;Assistant&lt;/h3&gt;&lt;div class=&quot;text&quot;&gt;&lt;pre&gt;I haven't run `max_dopant_sites.py` yet, because you asked me not to test. Treat it as untested until you've run it once.

**What it does:** it takes the 24d sites of your CIF, tiled over the supercell with `llzo_sites.wyckoff_sites`, so the site list and index order match your pipeline. It then finds the largest set of sites where no two dopants are closer than `dmin`. Distances use periodic boundaries, so a dopant also sees its own images.

**How to run it** (put it next to `llzo_sites.py`):
```
python max_dopant_sites.py --fold 1 1 2 --dmin 5.0
python max_dopant_sites.py --fold 2 2 2 --radius 3.4 --spread --out al24d
python max_dopant_sites.py --fold 1 1 2 --dmin 0        # just prints the 24d–24d distance shells
```
- **`--dmin` or `--radius`:** `--radius R` sets dmin = 2R, so the spheres of influence just touch.
- **`--spread`:** after the maximum n is found, it also finds the arrangement with that n and the largest minimum distance.
- **Exactness:** the exact solver (scipy `milp`, time-limited) says whether the optimum is proven. The greedy search is a fallback for large cells.

**Outputs:** `&amp;lt;out&amp;gt;_sites.csv` has the chosen sites with fractional and Cartesian coordinates and the site indices. `&amp;lt;out&amp;gt;.xyz` has the dopant positions with the lattice. The script also prints x per formula unit, Li_{7−3x}Al_xLa₃Zr₂O₁₂, and the volume per Al.

**Your cell description doesn't add up:**
- One conventional cell is Li56 La24 Zr16 O96.
- 192 O is two conventional cells, `--fold 1 1 2`, which has Li112 at full stoichiometry.
- &amp;quot;8×8×8&amp;quot; would be 512 cells with about 49,000 O.
- I defaulted to `--fold 1 1 2`; tell me what you actually mean.

**Box size:** if the box edge is shorter than dmin, even one dopant is too close to its own image. The script stops and says so. For r95 ≈ 6.8 Å, dmin = 13.6 Å is longer than the 13 Å cell edge, so you would need `--fold 2 2 2` or larger.

**Does packing give the optimal doping?** Not by itself. The packing limit is only a geometric upper bound on how many non-overlapping dopants fit. The conductivity optimum also depends on the Li vacancies each dopant creates, which raise conductivity, so it will usually sit below the packing limit. The radius also comes from your 2×2×4 runs and may change with concentration, so check that before treating it as fixed.

**A rough check against experiment:**
- At x = 0.25 Al per formula unit, each Al has about 1094 Å³, which is a sphere of radius about 6.4 Å.
- That is close to your r95 of 6.8 Å for Al, which would put the space-filling limit at about x ≈ 0.21.
- If the spheres must not overlap, the maximum is lower, about x ≈ 0.15.
- Reported optima are, from memory, around x ≈ 0.2–0.25 for Al and Ga, so check the exact values against papers.

It is a crude estimate, but the numbers are in the same range.

## What You Have (from your MD simulations)

| Quantity | Value | Source |
|----------|-------|--------|
| **r95 (Al)** | 6.77 Å | Your fitted sigmoid/MSD(r) profile |
| **y(r) = MSD(r)/MSD(far)** | Continuous function | Your Gaussian-kernel regression + sigmoid fit |
| **V_zone** | `∫[1 − y(r)] 4πr² dr` | **Compute this from your y(r)** |
| **Uncertainty** | Bootstrap CI on y(r) | Propagate to V_zone |

---

## What You Need (to predict optimal x)

Three-component model:
```
σ(x) = f_vacancy(x) × f_blocking(x) × f_site(x)
```

### Component 1: Vacancy Benefit (global)
```python
def f_vacancy(x):
    # Each Al³⁺ on Li⁺ creates 2 vacancies
    # Benefit saturates at high x
    return x / (x + x0)  # x0 ≈ 0.1 from literature fitting
```

### Component 2: Blocking Overlap (from YOUR data)
```python
def f_blocking(x):
    V_fu = 187.0  # Å³ per formula unit (cubic LLZO, a≈12.97Å, 8 f.u./cell)
    V_zone = compute_from_your_y(r)  # ← YOU COMPUTE THIS
    return max(0.0, 1.0 - x * V_zone / V_fu)  # mean-field, linear overlap
```

### Component 3: Site Crowding (literature)
```python
def f_site(x):
    # Li content = 7 - 3x
    # Diffusivity drops sharply when Li &lt; 6.3 (24d over-occupancy)
    # Strachan 2023, Chen 2017
    Li = 7.0 - 3.0 * x
    if Li &gt;= 6.3:
        return 1.0
    else:
        return Li / 6.3  # linear drop below threshold
```

---

## Bounds (NOT from your radius)

| Bound | Value | Origin |
|-------|-------|--------|
| **x_min** | 0.15–0.20 | Minimum Al to stabilize cubic phase (experiment) |
| **x_max** | 0.23–0.25 | Li &lt; 6.3 (site crowding) + LiAlO₂ side phase formation |

---

## Complete Script Template

```python
import numpy as np
from scipy.integrate import quad

# ============================================================
# 1. LOAD YOUR FITTED y(r) = MSD(r)/MSD(far)
# ============================================================
# Replace with your actual fitted function
# Example sigmoid: y(r) = A + (1-A) / (1 + exp((r - r50)/w))
def y_of_r(r, A=0.3, r50=3.5, w=1.2):
    &quot;&quot;&quot;Sigmoid fit: A = depth, r50 = midpoint, w = width&quot;&quot;&quot;
    return A + (1 - A) / (1 + np.exp((r - r50) / w))

# ============================================================
# 2. COMPUTE V_zone = ∫[1 - y(r)] 4πr² dr
# ============================================================
def compute_V_zone(y_func, r_max=20.0):
    integrand = lambda r: (1 - y_func(r)) * 4 * np.pi * r**2
    V_zone, err = quad(integrand, 0, r_max)
    return V_zone, err

V_zone, V_err = compute_V_zone(y_of_r)
print(f&quot;V_zone = {V_zone:.1f} ± {V_err:.1f} Å³&quot;)

# ============================================================
# 3. THREE-TERM MODEL
# ============================================================
V_fu = 187.0  # Å³ per formula unit

def f_vacancy(x, x0=0.1):
    return x / (x + x0)

def f_blocking(x, V_zone):
    return max(0.0, 1.0 - x * V_zone / V_fu)

def f_site(x):
    Li = 7.0 - 3.0 * x
    return 1.0 if Li &gt;= 6.3 else Li / 6.3

def sigma_rel(x, V_zone):
    return f_vacancy(x) * f_blocking(x, V_zone) * f_site(x)

# ============================================================
# 4. FIND OPTIMUM
# ============================================================
xs = np.linspace(0.15, 0.30, 151)
sigmas = [sigma_rel(x, V_zone) for x in xs]
x_opt = xs[np.argmax(sigmas)]
sigma_max = max(sigmas)

print(f&quot;\nOptimal Al content: x = {x_opt:.3f} (Li = {7-3*x_opt:.3f})&quot;)
print(f&quot;Relative conductivity at optimum: {sigma_max:.3f}&quot;)

# ============================================================
# 5. UNCERTAINTY PROPAGATION (optional)
# ============================================================
# Sample V_zone from bootstrap distribution of your y(r) fits
# Recompute x_opt for each sample → CI on x_opt
```

---

## Expected Result

With your **r95 = 6.77 Å**, typical sigmoid parameters give **V_zone ≈ 1000–1500 Å³**.

Plugging in:
- `V_zone ≈ 1300 Å³`
- `x0 = 0.1`

**Predicted x_opt ≈ 0.20–0.24** (Li ≈ 6.28–6.40)

This matches the experimental optimum **Al = 0.20–0.25**.

3. **Compare to experiment** — if x_opt ≈ 0.22, your radius + model is consistent

## Key Insight

&gt; **Your radius is NOT the optimal spacing.**  
&gt; It quantifies the **local blocking volume** (Component 2).  
&gt; The optimum emerges from **balancing all three components** — vacancy benefit (global) vs. blocking overlap (local, your data) vs. site crowding (global, literature).</pre></details><details class="attachment"><summary>Attachment: novelty_check.md</summary><pre># Literature Novelty Check: Radius of Influence of Dopants on Li Mobility in LLZO

## 1. Verdict in 3 Sentences

**(a) Prior work measuring Li dynamics as a function of distance from dopant:** Found extensive prior work. Multiple MD studies across garnet (LLZO), NASICON, and sulfide/halide electrolytes report radial MSD profiles, distance‑dependent diffusion coefficients, and/or radial occupancy profiles around dopants. The explicit combination of (i) continuous MSD(r) via Gaussian‑kernel regression, (ii) normalization by far‑Li MSD, (iii) a non‑dopant null‑control profile, and (iv) block‑bootstrap uncertainty is **not found** — individual ingredients exist but the full methodological pipeline is novel.

**(b) Prior work defining a numerical &quot;radius of influence&quot; / affected zone / trapping radius:** Found multiple papers giving numerical blocking/trapping radii (~3–4 Å for Ga/Al on Li24d sites in LLZO, ~2–5 Å in sulfides). The concept of a distance‑dependent suppression zone around a dopant is well‑established, but the specific metric of r95 (distance where deficit recovers to 95%) from a sigmoid/fit to a normalized MSD profile is not reported.

**(c) Prior work using such a radius to predict optimal doping concentration via overlap/percolation:** Found percolation/excluded‑volume models that set an upper dopant fraction (~10 at % total, Li content ~6.2–6.5 per formula unit) to stay below the 3‑D percolation threshold. However, these use fixed excluded‑volume spheres and do not connect a distance‑resolved MSD profile to a predicted optimal concentration — the specific coupling of r95/effective frozen‑sphere radius to concentration optimization is **not found**.

**(d) Same methodology applied to other mobile ions (O²⁻, Na⁺, H⁺) in oxides:** Found radial MSD/r occupancy analyses for O²⁻ in doped ceria/YSZ and Na⁺ in NASICON, with blocking shells and correlation lengths reported. The exact MSD(r) kernel‑regression + far‑Li normalization + bootstrap + sigmoid‑fit pipeline is **not found** for non‑Li mobile ions; analogous distance‑resolved analyses exist but with different methodology.

## 3. Closest Paper Deep Analysis: Gao, Jalem &amp; Tateyama 2022 (J. Mater. Chem. A 10, 10083)

**What it does:**
- Uses first‑principles DFT and classical MD to investigate how Al, Ga, Ta, Nb dopants segregate to grain boundaries (GBs) in Li₇La₃Zr₂O₁₂ (LLZO).
- Dopants preferentially occupy the 24d Li site at GBs, coordinating three Li next to Zr.
- Computes MSD of Li ions in doped GB regions vs. bulk, finding slower diffusion (lower D) at doped GBs.
- Generates Li–Li and Li–dopant radial distribution functions (RDFs), revealing a narrowed Li‑site population around dopants — i.e., restricted migration pathways.
- Reports that the Al³⁺/Ga³⁺ ionic radius (Al ≈ 0.53 Å, Ga ≈ 0.62 Å) distorts the local Li‑O polyhedron and raises the Li‑diffusion barrier.
- The paper provides a &quot;dopant‑impact map&quot; predicting optimal dopant choices for high‑conductivity cubic LLZO, but does **not** compute a continuous MSD(r) profile, does not normalize by far‑Li MSD, does not use a null‑control profile around non‑dopant reference points, and does not apply kernel regression, block bootstrap, or sigmoid/exponential/Gaussian fitting of a depth‑free plateau.

**What it does NOT do (compared to the proposed method):**
- Does **not** compute MSD as a continuous function of distance from each dopant origin point (r).
- Does **not** normalize the profile by the MSD of far Li (farther than ~7 Å from every dopant).
- Does **not** generate a control profile around non‑dopant Li‑site reference points (null band).
- Does **not** quantify uncertainty via block bootstrap over time blocks.
- Does **not** fit the profile with sigmoid/exponential/Gaussian functions to extract r50, r95, and suppression depth A.
- Does **not** compute an &quot;effective frozen‑sphere radius&quot; from the fit.
- The radius reported (~4 Å blocking shell for Ga/Al on 24d sites) is a qualitative/visual cutoff, not a fitted parameter from a normalized MSD profile.

**Bottom line:** The Gao et al. paper provides the foundational atomistic context — showing that dopants at Li24d sites create local Li‑site blocking and distortion — but it does not implement the continuous distance‑resolved MSD regression, normalization, null control, bootstrap, or fitted radius methodology that the proposed analysis introduces.

---

## 4. Which Individual Ingredients of the Method Have Precedent?

| Ingredient | Prior work with precedent? | Reference(s) | How it compares to the proposed method |
|------------|----------------------------|--------------|----------------------------------------|
| **Kernel regression of a property vs. distance** | Yes — Gaussian process (GP) regression / sparse GP used to model Li diffusivity as a function of compositional/structural descriptors including Li‑dopant distance (ArXiv 2009.13179; BIRD data‑mining). | — | GP/sparse GP predicts a scalar diffusivity or activation energy from descriptors; the proposed method uses kernel regression on a 1‑D continuous MSD(r) curve fitted per‑dopant, not a global surrogate model. |
| **Null‑control profile around non‑dopant reference points** | Partial — some papers compute MSD or occupancy around generic &quot;reference Li sites&quot; or &quot;far‑Li&quot; to define a baseline, but not explicitly as a null band subtracted from the dopant‑proximal profile. | de Klerk et al. 2018 (sulfide): occupancy maps with a distance cutoff; Verduzco et al. 2023: MSD of undoped LLZO as reference. | The proposed method explicitly contrasts the dopant‑proximal MSD(r) against a control profile centered on non‑dopant Li‑site reference points, providing a direct &quot;deficit&quot; measurement. |
| **Block bootstrap over time blocks for uncertainty** | Rare in LLZO MD literature — most studies report standard errors from block‑averaged MSD or from multiple independent trajectories, but not a formal block‑bootstrap resampling of the MSD(r) profile. | Strachan group papers often use block‑averaged MSD; some newer papers mention bootstrap but not for distance‑resolved profiles. | The proposed method's block‑bootstrap of the entire MSD(r) curve (preserving temporal correlations) appears to be a more rigorous uncertainty quantification than simple block averaging. |
| **Sigmoid / exponential / Gaussian fit with free plateau to extract r50, r95, depth A** | Not found in LLZO literature — fits to MSD profiles typically use linear regimes (MSD ∝ t) to extract diffusion coefficients, not distance‑dependent sigmoidal fits. | — | The proposed method's fitting of a sigmoid (etc.) to MSD(r) with a free plateau to define r95 (distance where deficit recovers to 95%) and r50 appears novel for this system. |
| **Effective frozen‑sphere radius computation** | Partial — &quot;blocking shell&quot; / &quot;trapping radius&quot; ≈ 3–4 Å reported qualitatively for Ga/Al on Li24d sites (Garcia Daza et al. 2019; multiple follow‑ups), but not computed as a fitted parameter from a normalized, bootstrapped MSD(r) profile. | Garcia Daza et al. 2019 (blocking ~4 Å); multiple MD studies reporting dopant‑proximal vacancy trapping. | The proposed method's &quot;effective frozen‑sphere radius&quot; is derived from the fitted sigmoid profile, giving a quantitative, uncertainty‑quantified radius rather than a visual/heuristic cutoff. |
| **Comparing radius across dopants, temperatures, and lag times** | Yes — multiple papers report MSD‑derived diffusion coefficients or activation energies as functions of dopant type and temperature. | Strachan group (2023 arXiv), Verduzco et al. (2023 arXiv), Lopez‑Puga et al. (2024). | The proposed method adds the dimension of a distance‑resolved radius (r50, r95) that can be tracked as a function of T and τ, which is not done in prior work. |

**Overall assessment:** The proposed method combines several ingredients that have appeared separately in the literature (kernel regression, MSD profiling, block averaging, qualitative blocking radii, percolation models), but **the specific combination of all of them — continuous MSD(r) via Gaussian‑kernel regression, normalization by far‑Li MSD, a non‑dopant null control, block‑bootstrap uncertainty, and sigmoid/exponential/Gaussian fitting with extracted r50/r95/A, plus comparison across dopants/temperatures/lag times — has not been done before.**

## 6. Final Confidence Statement

**How sure am I that nothing identical exists?**  
I am **high-confidence (≈ 85 %)** that no paper implements the exact combined methodology described: continuous MSD(r) via Gaussian‑kernel regression, normalization by far‑Li MSD, a non‑dopant null‑control profile, block‑bootstrap uncertainty quantification, and sigmoid/exponential/Gaussian fitting with extracted r50/r95/suppression‑depth A, applied across multiple dopants (Al, Ga, Fe, Mg, Zn), temperatures, and lag times in garnet LLZO. 

**What would raise that confidence to &gt; 95 %?**  
1. Direct confirmation from the original researchers (e.g., Yoshitaka Tateyama, Alejandro Strachan, or the Vema group) that they have not published this exact pipeline.  
2. A targeted search of specific databases (Web of Science, Scopus) using the exact combination of MeSH/keyword terms: `&quot;mean squared displacement&quot; AND &quot;distance&quot; AND &quot;dopant&quot; AND &quot;kernel regression&quot; AND &quot;solid electrolyte&quot;`.  
3. Checking the published datasets/code repositories (e.g., Materials Project, QuantumATK user communities) for any code that computes distance‑resolved MSD with kernel regression and bootstrap uncertainty.  
4. A specific request from an experimentalist or battery company looking to validate or build on this methodology — if they cite prior art, that would either confirm or refute novelty.

**Most likely scenario:** The individual ingredients (distance‑resolved MSD profiles, kernel regression for property smoothing, block bootstrap for uncertainty, sigmoid fitting for radius extraction, percolation‑based optimal doping models) are all well‑established in the broader solid‑electrolyte MD literature, but **no single paper has assembled all of them into the proposed workflow.** The proposed method represents a novel synthesis of existing techniques applied to a new question (radius of influence of specific dopants on Li mobility in LLZO, compared across charge/ionic radius/Slater effective charge).</pre></details></section><section class="msg assistant"><h3>Assistant</h3><div class="text"><pre>**Short answer:** the math has errors. **Errors**

1. **V_fu wrong.** 187 Å³ is not a formula unit. Cell = 2188.24 Å³, Z = 8, so V_fu = **273.5 Å³**. This also contradicts my earlier 1094 Å³ per Al at x = 0.25.
2. **y(r) example is inverted.** `A + (1-A)/(1+exp((r-r50)/w))` gives y(0) ≈ 1 and y(∞) = A. That is a dip at far distance. Your fit form is `B(1 - A/(1+exp((r-r0)/w)))`: dip at the dopant, y → B far away. Use that.
3. **V_zone ≈ 1000–1500 Å³ is the full sphere at r95.** 4/3·π·6.77³ = 1300 Å³. That assumes fully frozen Li (y = 0) inside. Your depth is A ≈ 0.54, not 1. Correct integral for the sigmoid (B = 1):
   V_zone = 4π·A·(r0³/3 + π²w²r0/3)
   Rough guess r0 ≈ 4.7, w ≈ 0.9 (derived from r95 only, unsure) gives about **300 Å³**. Use your fitted r0 and w, not my guess. Use `r_eff`/`V_A3` from `radius_summary.csv` if you want a model-free number.
4. **&quot;x_opt ≈ 0.20–0.24&quot; is unsupported.**
   - With V_fu = 273.5 and V_zone = 1300: f_blocking = 1 − 4.75x, and the product peaks near x ≈ 0.07. That is below x_min, so the grid 0.15–0.30 just returns the boundary 0.15.
   - With V_zone ≈ 300 the product peaks near x ≈ 0.21. That agrees with experiment, but only because x0 = 0.1 is tuned. Move x0 and the optimum moves. It is a free parameter, not a prediction.
5. **x0 = 0.1 &quot;from literature fitting&quot;: unverified.** I can't confirm it. Treat it as free.
6. **f_site and the Li &lt; 6.3 threshold, Strachan 2023 / Chen 2017:** unsure. I can't confirm the 6.3 value or that those papers give it. A threshold and a linear drop are ad hoc.
7. **f_blocking = 1 − xV/V_fu is first order only.** Zones overlap, so the covered fraction is more like 1 − exp(−x·V/V_fu) (random placement). It also makes conductivity linear in mean mobility, which ignores percolation of the unblocked Li paths.
8. **f_vacancy is not physical.** A lattice gas with c = (7−3x)/15 (24d + 96h = 15 sites per formula unit) gives σ ∝ c(1−c), which peaks at Li = 7.5. Al removes Li, so that term falls with x. The real benefit of Al comes from stabilizing the cubic, disordered phase (the x_min bound), not from a smooth x/(x+x0).

**Has it been done?**

- The qualitative idea exists. Strachan 2023 (arXiv:2305.08839) is, per the novelty check, about vacancy concentration vs site blocking giving a diffusivity maximum. Percolation / excluded-volume models of optimal doping also exist.
- A model that takes a measured, distance-resolved MSD(r) radius and depth and derives x_opt from them was **not found** in `novelty_check.md`. That is &quot;not found&quot;, not &quot;does not exist&quot;.
- That report is weak. It contradicts itself on whether numerical blocking radii exist. Rows 4, 10, 11 and 12 are duplicates or off-topic. &quot;Full text read&quot; is claimed for papers it only partly accessed. Redo the search before claiming novelty.

**What I'd do**

- Replace V_zone with the depth-weighted one from your fit. Propagate the bootstrap CI.
- Treat x0 and the site term as unknowns. Show x_opt vs x0 as a band, not a point.
- Test the model against your own data: simulate Al at 2 to 3 concentrations and check whether MSD drops as the overlap model predicts. If r95 changes with concentration, the &quot;intrinsic radius&quot; assumption fails.
- Do Ga, Fe, Mg and Zn the same way. Rank V_zone across them. That ranking is a more robust claim than an absolute x_opt.

Corrected core, to drop in:

```python
def y_of_r(r, A, r0, w, B=1.0):
    return B * (1 - A / (1 + np.exp((r - r0) / w)))

V_fu = 2188.24 / 8   # 273.5 A^3
V_zone = quad(lambda r: (1 - y_of_r(r, A, r0, w)) * 4*np.pi*r**2, 0, 20)[0]
f_block = lambda x: np.exp(-x * V_zone / V_fu)
```

## Model after fixes

σ(x) ∝ n_Li(x) · D_far(x) · exp(−x · V_A / V_fu)

- V_fu = 273.5 Å³ (12.98³ / 8).
- V_A = ∫(1 − y) 4πr² dr, per dopant. Use model-free `V_A3`. It carries depth A, so no full sphere.
- Block term is exact if dopants land at random (Poisson) and zones multiply. Then ⟨∏y⟩ = exp(−ρ∫(1−y)dV). Both assumptions are unverified. 24d sites are not random. Item 7 tests multiplication.
- n_Li = 7 − q·x. D_far(x) = vacancy benefit, from item 4.

Optimum: d ln D_far/dx = V_A/V_fu + q/(7 − qx)

**Result.** Al block slope V_A/V_fu ≈ 1.0–1.7 per unit x. At x = 0.2 block factor is 0.71–0.82. Blocking is gentle. It cannot make a sharp peak. Optimum comes from D_far(x) curvature plus phase limits. Radius alone predicts nothing about x_opt. It predicts a ranking: cost slope Al &gt; Ga &gt; Fe &gt; Zn &gt;&gt; Mg. That ranking is the robust claim. Confidence moderate, since w is guessed.

Assistant

**The plan, in one line:** measure how much each dopant slows nearby Li. Combine that with how many Li vacancies the dopant creates. Predict conductivity vs doping amount.

## The math

σ(x) ∝ n_Li · D_vac(n_Li) · exp(−x · V_A / V_fu)

Three parts:

1. **n_Li = 7 − q·x.** Li left in the cell.
   - q = 3 for Al, Ga, Fe.
   - q = 2 for Mg, Zn.
2. **D_vac(n_Li).** Benefit side: more vacancies, easier Li hopping.
   - Same for all dopants (assumption, tested).
   - Measured from far-away Li, with Al calibrating it.
3. **exp(−x · V_A / V_fu).** Cost side: each dopant slows Li around it.
   - V_A = size of the slowed zone, counting how much it slows. From your radius profile. One number per dopant.
   - V_fu = 273.5 Å³, the volume per formula unit.
   - Exact only if dopants sit at random and their zones multiply. Both are tested, not assumed.

Optimum is where benefit growth equals blocking cost:

d ln D_vac / dx = V_A/V_fu + q/(7 − qx)

## What each part is

- **Cost:** you already have it (V_A from `radius_summary.csv`).
- **Benefit:** measure from your 0, 1/16, 2/16, 3/16 runs.
- **Peak position:** comes mostly from the benefit curve, not the radius. My rough estimate (w guessed): blocking only costs ~20–30% at x = 0.2. So the radius does not predict x_opt alone.

## What you get

- Relative conductivity curve σ(x)/σ(pure) per dopant. Not S/cm.
- x_opt with an uncertainty band.
- Blocking cost per dopant, ranked: Al > Ga > Fe > Zn >> Mg (if V_A holds up).
- A test of whether the benefit is the same for all dopants. If yes, one Al calibration serves all.
- Plot of radius vs x, to see if the radius is intrinsic.

## What you do not get

- Not "any dopant, any concentration". Only the tested range.
- No absolute conductivity (needs Haven ratio and calibration).
- No phase stability or solubility limits (cubic vs tetragonal, LiAlO₂).
- No grain boundaries. Experiment has them, MD does not.
- No dopant site preference. Fe, Zn, Mg may not sit on Li sites.

## Runs needed

- **Existing runs:** cover the tests (closure, collapse, hold-out).
- **One new run:** Al at 5/16. It goes past the expected peak and tests the prediction.
- **New dopant:** one dilute run gives V_A. No high-x run if the collapse test passes.

## Honest status

It is a hypothesis with built-in tests, not a result yet. If the collapse test fails, each dopant needs its own high-x run. If dopants are not random on their sites, the exp() rule may be off. The 5/16 run or a second arrangement would show that.

User

D_vac(n_Li )  is a function of n_li ...not multiplication right?also since this is a exponential decay, it assumes no phase change and doping always reduces ionic cond.....right?..also x is Li or dopant concentration?

also why this equation?....where did theoptima eqution comefrom.....who said it

Assistant

**1. D_vac(n_Li): function, not multiplication.**
Yes, you are right. D_vac(n_Li) means "the mobility measured at Li content n_Li". It is a curve you measure, not a number I multiply by n_Li. The only multiplication is between the three finished terms: n_Li × D_vac value at that n_Li × exp term.

**2. Does the exponential mean doping always lowers conductivity?**
- The exp term alone always falls with x. It is only the blocking cost.
- The total can still rise at low x, because D_vac(n_Li) rises as Li vacancies appear. The peak is where the two fight.
- Phase change is **not** in the model. Your MD "pure" run is forced cubic. Real undoped LLZO is tetragonal and poorly conducting. In experiment, Al's biggest gain is that it stabilizes the cubic phase. The model cannot see that. It only describes doping inside the cubic phase.

**3. Is x the Li or the dopant concentration?**
x is the dopant amount per formula unit: Li₇₋qₓ M ₓ La₃Zr₂O₁₂. Your 1/16 means x = 0.0625 Al atoms per formula unit. Li content is then 7 − q·x.

**4. Where does the equation come from, and who said it?**
Nobody. I derived it. I know of no paper that has it. Two steps:

- **Blocking term.** Each Li's mobility is the product of the slowdown factors y(r) from all dopants near it. If dopants sit at random, the average of that product over the cell is exp(−ρ·V_A). Here ρ = x/V_fu is the dopant density and V_A = ∫(1 − y)·4πr² dr. This is a standard result for random points. The assumptions are mine: random placement, and zones that multiply. I test both. They are not proven.
- **Optimum.** This is plain calculus. Take ln σ = ln n_Li + ln D_vac − x·V_A/V_fu. Set the derivative with respect to x to zero. Using n_Li = 7 − q·x, the derivative of ln n_Li is −q/(7 − qx). That gives:

  d ln D_vac/dx = V_A/V_fu + q/(7 − qx)

  Left side is the benefit of more vacancies. Right side is the blocking cost plus the loss of Li carriers.

The model is my hypothesis, built from standard pieces. The closure and collapse tests, and the 5/16 run, are there to show whether it holds. Do not call it established until they pass.

User

no no no....what?......explain the blobking term...and its not possible that u derived smtg which doesnt exist...so.....find out where u pulled it  from

Assistant

You're right to push. I overstated "nobody". I didn't invent the math, I assembled it from known results. I can't point to one paper I took it from, so treat the names below as pointers to check.

**Blocking term, simple words**

Each dopant slows Li near it. Far away the slowdown is nothing (factor 1). Close up it is strong (factor y < 1). Your fitted y(r) gives this factor at every distance.

A Li near several dopants gets slowed by each one. I assumed the slowdowns multiply: y₁ × y₂ × y₃...

Now scatter the dopants at random in the cell. The average slowdown over all Li is:

exp( −(dopant density) × V_A )

- Dopant density = x / V_fu (dopants per Å³).
- V_A = ∫(1 − y) 4πr² dr. This is the slowed volume, counting depth. A zone where Li is 50% slower counts as half a frozen sphere.

Check with a simple case. If every zone is a fully frozen sphere (y = 0), the formula gives the chance that a Li has no dopant within the sphere. That is the Poisson void probability, exp(−density × volume). Partial slowdown just generalizes it.

**Where this comes from**

- It is the probability generating functional of a Poisson point process (Campbell's theorem; see Kingman, *Poisson Processes*). This is the same statistics you get from random obstacles, the "Swiss cheese" model.
- The same form appears in Förster energy transfer and trapping theory: survival with randomly placed acceptors is exp(−ρ∫(1 − survival(r)) dV). I'm fairly sure of this, not certain of the exact papers.

**What is mine, not from literature**

- Using it for Li MSD in LLZO, with y(r) from your kernel-regression profile.
- Assuming mobility is the product of the zone factors. Your overlap test checks this.
- Assuming dopants sit at random. Real 24d sites are ordered, so this may fail.

I haven't searched for a paper doing this exact use. I can search if you want citations. Then you'd know whether it is truly new or just a known formula applied to a new problem.

User

why is the average an exponential?

Assistant

Short version: the exponential comes from "no dopant nearby" being a chance event that multiplies across independent regions of space.

**Step 1: one dopant.** Take one Li. A dopant lands at random somewhere in the cell. Its slowdown on that Li depends on distance. Averaged over where it could land, the average slowdown factor is

1 − (V_A / V_cell)

V_A is the "slowed volume" and V_cell is the whole cell. So one dopant removes a small fraction V_A/V_cell.

**Step 2: N dopants, independent.** Each dopant is placed independently. If slowdowns multiply, the average over all N is the product of N identical averages:

(1 − V_A/V_cell)^N

**Step 3: many dopants, big cell.** N = x · (number of formula units), and V_cell = (number of formula units) · V_fu. So N/V_cell = x/V_fu, the dopant density. Use the standard limit (1 − a/N)^N → e^(−a):

(1 − V_A/V_cell)^N → exp(−N · V_A / V_cell) = exp(−x · V_A / V_fu)

That is the exponential. It is the same reason radioactive decay is exponential, and the same reason "chance of no raindrop hitting you" is exponential.

**Why not linear?**
The linear version, 1 − x·V_A/V_fu, is the first term of the same series. It counts each dopant's slowdown and treats them as separate. But when zones overlap, a Li already slowed by one dopant can't be "slowed again by the full amount" in a subtractive way. Multiplying slowdowns keeps the result above zero. The linear form goes negative at large x, which is impossible. At small x (x·V_A/V_fu small) both agree.

**What the exponential assumes (the weak points):**
1. Dopants land at random, independent of each other. Real 24d sites are an ordered lattice, so dopants can't land closer than the lattice allows. That makes the real curve differ from the exponential at high x.
2. Slowdowns multiply. This is untested. Your overlap test checks it.

Your 1/16, 2/16 and 3/16 runs are meant to test both. If the exponential is right, plotting ln(whole-cell MSD / far MSD) against x should give a straight line with slope −V_A/V_fu.

User

why is slowdoen factor 1-va/vfu ...why not just va/vfu

Assistant

Because V_A/V_cell is the **lost** part, not the **left-over** part.

**Simple picture.** Take one Li at a random spot. A dopant sits at a random place in the cell.
- Chance the dopant is within the slowed zone: about V_A / V_cell.
- If it is within the zone, the Li is slowed. If not, the Li is untouched (factor 1).

So V_A/V_cell is how much mobility is **removed**. The slowdown factor is what **remains**:

factor = 1 − V_A/V_cell

**Check with numbers.** Say V_A/V_cell = 0.1.
- Factor = 0.9 means Li keep 90% of their mobility, lose 10%. That matches "10% of the cell is slowed".
- Factor = 0.1 would mean Li keep only 10%. That is the opposite.

**Another check.** No dopant: V_A = 0. 1 − 0 = 1, so mobility is unchanged (right). Using V_A/V_cell alone gives 0, meaning zero mobility with no dopant (wrong).

**Note on "slowdown factor".** My wording was loose. "Factor" here means the **fraction of mobility kept**. The **slowdown** is the other part, V_A/V_cell.

**What V_A means.** It is not just a volume. It is volume × depth: ∫(1 − y) dV. A zone where Li is 50% slower counts as half its volume. So V_A/V_cell is the average fraction of mobility lost, and 1 − V_A/V_cell is the average fraction kept.

User

wait.....N is total number of dopants...how is N= x* number of formula units?

Assistant

Because x is defined as dopants **per formula unit**.

Li₇₋₃ₓ Alₓ La₃Zr₂O₁₂ is one formula unit. It contains x Al atoms. So:

N (Al atoms) = x × (number of formula units in the cell)

**Your numbers.** One conventional cell = 8 formula units (Z = 8).
- x = 1/16 → 8 × 1/16 = **0.5 Al per conventional cell**.
- Your 2x2x4 cell = 16 conventional cells = **128 formula units**.
- So N = 128 × 1/16 = **8 Al**. For 2/16: 16 Al. For 3/16: 24 Al.

I'm assuming your cell is 2x2x4 conventional cells (from the folder name). If it isn't, check the Al count in your dump. N is just what the dump says.

**Why this matters for the formula.**
- V_cell = (formula units) × V_fu = 128 × 273.5 Å³.
- N / V_cell = x × 128 / (128 × 273.5) = x / V_fu.

The number of formula units cancels. So the exponent depends only on x, not on cell size. That is why the model can predict for larger supercells too.

**Quick check on your data.** Count Al atoms in each folder, divide by formula units. It should give 1/16, 2/16, 3/16. If it gives something else, the x values in `doping_map.csv` need fixing.

One correction to my earlier answer: in the 2x2x4 cell with 1/16, 8 Al is also why the dopant spacing matters. The cell volume per Al is 128 × 273.5 / 8 ≈ 4376 Å³ at 1/16, which is a sphere of radius about 10 Å. That is close to your 6.8 Å radius, so overlap is already real at 1/16, not just at high x.

User

okay i got the exponential prt....what abt the n_li and Dvac?

Assistant

## n_Li: how many Li are left

n_Li = 7 − q·x. It is Li per formula unit.

Why Li is lost: charge balance. Al³⁺ takes a Li⁺ site, and the cell needs to stay neutral.
- Al³⁺ takes 1 Li site. Its extra +2 charge needs 2 Li⁺ removed. Total Li lost = **3 per Al**, so q = 3.
- Mg²⁺ takes 1 Li site, and 1 Li is removed for charge. q = 2.
- Same for the others: Ga, Fe use q = 3. Zn uses q = 2.

Example, Al at x = 1/16: n_Li = 7 − 3/16 = 6.81.

Why multiply by n_Li: conductivity = (number of carriers) × (how mobile each is). Li are the carriers. This is the Nernst-Einstein idea, σ ∝ n·D. It ignores the Haven ratio and holds T fixed. So n_Li is a stand-in for carrier count.

## D_vac: how easily Li move

D_vac(n_Li) is the mobility of Li that no dopant is slowing, at a given Li content.

Why it depends on n_Li: the lattice has 15 Li sites per formula unit (24d + 96h) and only 7 Li on them. Remove Li and there are more empty sites. Li can hop more easily. More vacancies, higher D_vac, up to a point.

So the two terms fight:
- n_Li **falls** as x goes up (fewer carriers).
- D_vac **rises** as x goes up (easier hopping).

The peak is where they balance, together with the blocking cost.

## How D_vac is measured

- Use far-Li MSD from your runs. Far Li are more than 7 Å from every dopant, so the blocking zone does not slow them.
- Plot far-Li MSD against n_Li. Al points: n_Li = 7 (pure), 6.81, 6.63, 6.44. That is only 4 points.
- Fit ln D vs n_Li, once straight and once curved. Show both.
- Calibrate with Al. Then use the same curve for Ga, Fe, Mg, Zn, if they fall on it. That is the collapse test.

## Weak points

- **Far Li vanish at high x.** At 3/16 the dopants are about 10 Å apart, so few Li are truly far. D_vac gets noisy and may still carry some dopant effect.
- **Narrow range.** n_Li only goes 7 to 6.44. Your peak is expected near 6.3 to 6.4, so you have to extrapolate. That is why the 5/16 run (n_Li = 6.06) is worth doing.
- **MSD at one τ is not D.** The ratios shift if τ is not in the diffusive regime.
- **q assumes dopant on a Li site.** Unsure for Fe, Zn, Mg in real samples.
- **Pure run is forced cubic.** Real undoped LLZO is tetragonal. D_vac at n_Li = 7 is the cubic value, not the real material.

D_vac is the part the model does not predict. It is measured, then extended. The blocking term only corrects it.

dude....first tell me how tf did u do this......like......damn.....but idk if i can trust this dude

Assistant

Fair. Don't trust it blindly. Here is how I built it, and how sure I am of each piece.

**How I built it**
1. Your own data gave the cost side. Your radius work produces y(r), the profile of how much Li slows near a dopant. That is the real measurement.
2. I took a standard piece of statistics for random obstacles: if you scatter points at random and each one reduces something by a factor, the average over space is exp(−density × ∫(1−y)dV). I rebuilt this from the (1 − a/N)^N limit in my last answer. You followed it, so you can check it yourself.
3. I took the textbook idea that conductivity ∝ carriers × mobility, with carriers set by charge balance (Li lost per dopant).
4. Calculus on the product gives the optimum equation.

No step is exotic. It is assembly, not discovery. That is why it looks clean.

**Where it is solid**
- Charge balance (q = 3 or 2): standard chemistry.
- The exponential, *if* dopants are random and zones multiply: standard math.
- The optimum equation: calculus.
- V_fu = 273.5 Å³: from your CIF (2188.24 / 8).

**Where it is not solid (my mistakes or guesses)**
- I earlier said "nobody has this" and then admitted I know the pieces. I did not search for a paper using it. So "new" or "known" is unchecked.
- Random placement is wrong for 24d sites, which are an ordered lattice. The exponential may be off.
- Zones multiplying is an assumption. Your 1/16, 2/16, 3/16 data tests it.
- D_vac is not predicted. It is measured from 4 points, then extended.
- My numbers for V_A (like 280-475 Å³, "blocking costs 20-30%") used a guessed width w. Those are estimates, not results.
- Earlier I gave a rough 6.8 Å vs 6.4 Å sphere argument, and in my last answer I said overlap is "already real at 1/16". That is hand arithmetic, unchecked.

**How to decide if you can trust it**
Don't trust me, trust the tests. They are built to fail if I'm wrong:
- ln(whole-cell MSD / far MSD) vs x should be a straight line. If not, the exponential is wrong.
- Al, Ga, Fe, Mg, Zn should collapse onto one D_vac curve vs n_Li. If not, the host-property assumption is wrong.
- Predict 2/16 and 3/16 from 1/16. Compare to measured.
- The 5/16 run predicts before you see it.

If those pass, the model earns trust from your data. If they fail, you learn exactly which assumption broke. Also check the derivations by hand, and ask me to search the literature for the exact formula.

Assistant

Thinking


## Short answer

I found papers on each piece, but none that put them together this way. "Not found" is not "doesn't exist". I only read abstracts and snippets, not full papers.

## n_Li × D(n_Li): papers that do this

- **Lattice-gas Monte Carlo on garnet (arXiv 1707.00491).** For non-interacting particles, conductivity goes with the mole fraction of mobile ions, which would put the maximum at x_Li = 4.5 for garnets. The correlation factor itself depends on concentration, so the best composition depends on the interaction details. This is the same "carriers × mobility" structure as my model.
- **Verduzco, Marinero, Strachan (arXiv 2305.08839).** Diffusivity peaks at Li content 6.3–6.5. The peak comes from vacancies rising while low-energy tetrahedral sites fill up, which raises the activation energy. This is the D(n_Li) curve I called D_vac. They also say Li concentration matters more than dopant chemistry at high temperature. That supports the "same curve for all dopants" idea.
- **Thompson et al., "A Tale of Two Sites".** The effective carrier concentration depends on Li site occupancy, and this explains the maximum near Li = 6.5.
- **arXiv 1506.07142.** It says Li concentration and arrangement mainly control transport in garnets.

The name "D_vac" is mine. The literature calls it D(Li content) or effective carrier concentration.

## Papers that go against my model

- **Fu et al. 2026** (the one with Al, Fe, Ga, Zn). Trivalent dopants help conductivity by making active vacancies. Zn²⁺ mostly makes vacancies next to the dopant, and those vacancies cannot move. Conductivity depends on how Li and vacancies spread over 24d and 96h sites, not on total vacancy count. So "one D_vac curve for all dopants" may fail, especially for Zn and Mg.
- **SINTEF study.** The best Al content is x = 0.2, the lowest amount that stabilizes cubic. Conductivity falls at higher doping, and the authors argue the dopant's main job is cubic stabilization, not vacancy creation. Parascos et al. also report a maximum at the critical cubic composition, x_c = 0.18. This is why the experimental peak is likely set by phase stability. My model cannot see that.

## The blocking term

- I found papers on **random obstacles and diffusion**. Effective diffusion among random overlapping obstacles follows a two-parameter power law over nearly the whole range of excluded volume, except near the percolation threshold. That is not a pure exponential.
- Hilfer and Orbach's work on dynamic percolation, where one random component blocks a second carrier component is the closest to dopants blocking ions.
- I did **not** find a paper that uses exp(−ρ·V_A) with an MD-measured y(r).
- Correction to my earlier answer: I said the exponential comes from Campbell's theorem and Förster transfer. That was from memory, and the search did not confirm it.
- **Important distinction.** The exponential is the *average* of the local slowdown over Li. Your whole-cell MSD at a fixed τ is exactly that average, so the closure test is fair. But long-time diffusion through a blocked medium is an effective-medium problem, not a plain average. At high x the exponential could be off.

## From Nernst-Einstein

Start with the standard relation:

σ = n · e² · D_σ / (kT)

Step by step:
1. n = Li per volume = n_Li / V_fu.
2. D_σ = D* / H_R (D* is the tracer diffusion from MSD, H_R is the Haven ratio).
3. So σ = (n_Li / V_fu) · e² · D* / (H_R · kT).
4. Take the ratio to the pure run at the same T:

σ(x)/σ(0) = [n_Li(x) / 7] · [D*(x) / D*(0)] · [H_R(0) / H_R(x)]

5. My model fills in D*(x) = D_vac(n_Li) · exp(−x·V_A/V_fu).

This shows where my model is thin:
- **H_R(x) is assumed constant.** It is not. The lattice-gas paper says the correlation factor depends on concentration. Your `03` script gives H_R, but with about 40% error.
- **Volume change** with x is ignored. It is small.
- **D_vac is not a free extra factor.** It is the n_Li-dependence of D*, so the model splits D* into a host part and a blocking part.

## What I would change

1. Stop treating D_vac as one curve for all dopants. Test it, and expect Zn and Mg to fail it.
2. Add the cubic stability limit as a hard bound. Experiment says the peak sits near the minimum Al that gives cubic.
3. Compare the exponential with the linear and power-law forms. The data can decide.
4. Include H_R from `03`, even though it is noisy.

Sources:
- [Lattice-Geometry Effects in Garnet Solid Electrolytes (arXiv 1707.00491)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1707.00491)
- [Atomistic mechanisms underlying the maximum in diffusivity in doped LLZO (arXiv 2305.08839)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2305.08839)
- [Effects of sublattice symmetry and frustration on ionic transport in garnet solid electrolytes (arXiv 1506.07142)](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1506.07142)
- [Influence of Li-site dopants in high-entropy Li-garnet (Fu et al., 2026)](https://pubs.rsc.org/en/content/articlehtml/2026/ta/d5ta10347a)
- [Optimal Al content in LLZO (SINTEF)](https://www.sintef.no/en/publications/publication/0198cc46ff3c-edf249ae-2e63-4621-bd01-01806d0e2f20/)
- [Optimizing Li+ transport in LLZO (Parascos et al.)](https://impact.ornl.gov/en/publications/optimizing-lisupsup-transport-in-lisub7sublasub3subzrsub2subosub1/)
- [Diffusion amid random overlapping obstacles](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3094463)
- [Hilfer and Orbach, Continuous time random walk approach to dynamic percolation](https://www2.icp.uni-stuttgart.de/~hilfer/publikationen/pdfo/ChemPhys-1988-128-275.pdf)

Assistant

**Yes, D_vac is Li diffusivity.** It is the tracer diffusivity D* (MSD/6t) of Li that no dopant is slowing, measured at a given Li content. "Vacancy" in the name just says it changes with vacancy count.

**Your point about vacancies sitting near dopants is a real problem for the model.**
- If vacancies stay trapped next to dopants, then the "active" vacancies are fewer than the 3x that charge balance gives.
- The trapped fraction depends on the dopant. So D_vac(n_Li) can shift from one dopant to another.
- It can also double count. Your y(r) already includes the slowdown from trapped vacancies near the dopant. Then the exp term and D_vac both carry part of the same effect.
- So V_A is **not** the only thing that changes between dopants. D_vac may change too. The Fu et al. paper says the same, just more strongly for Zn.

**What the model gives you**

σ(x)/σ(0) = (n_Li/7) · D_vac(n_Li) · exp(−x·V_A/V_fu)

- n_Li: calculated from charge balance.
- V_A: measured from your radius profile.
- D_vac: measured from your runs, not predicted. It is a fitted curve with 4 points.

So it is **not** a first-principles equation for conductivity vs dopant and stoichiometry. It is a semi-empirical form with one curve you calibrate on Al, and one cost number per dopant. It only becomes "one equation for all dopants" if the collapse test passes. Your own point makes me think it may not pass cleanly.

**It also leaves out three things**
- Cubic phase stability, which sets the real experimental peak.
- Haven ratio changes with x.
- Grain boundaries.

**Is it worth it?**

Yes, but only the cheap part first.
- Steps 1–4 need no new runs: radius vs x, whole-cell MSD vs x, the closure test and the collapse test. They take days of scripting, not weeks of compute.
- Their result decides everything. If the exponential closure test and the collapse both pass, the model earns the 5/16 run. If they fail, stop and you've lost little.
- What you gain even if the full model fails: V_A and the radius per dopant are a real measured result. The novelty check says the distance-resolved radius itself appears not to have been published. That is worth more than the optimum equation.
- What I would not do: sell it as "predicts conductivity for any dopant". It predicts a trend, and only inside the range you test.

If you want a plain, safe fallback: just plot measured whole-cell MSD vs x for each dopant. The model only adds value if it lets you predict a dopant or concentration you did not run.

now why are there 2 formulae?...put them in the plan dude...put all that we discussed in to the plan.....so the problem is haven ratio. since the haven ratio can also depend on the R_A, can we fit one Hr_vs R_A per dopant, and get some relation, so that it can be approximated at least?

Assistant

Updated plan is in `doping_model_plan.md`. It now holds everything we discussed.

**Why two formulae**
- **A, Nernst-Einstein:** σ = n·e²·D*/(H_R·kT). This is the textbook definition of σ.
- **B, my model for D\*:** D* = D_vac(n_Li)·exp(−x·V_A/V_fu). This is the hypothesis to test.
- They don't compete. A says what σ is, B says what goes inside D*. Put B into A and you get the model.
- The optimum equation is a third thing, derived by calculus from A and B.

**Can H_R be fitted against V_A per dopant? Not really.**
- 03 gives one H_R per run, with about 40% error. Per dopant you have only 4 points (0, 1/16, 2/16, 3/16). A trend would vanish in the noise.
- Pool all dopants and temperatures instead. Fit ln H_R against two things: how many Li are removed (7 − n_Li) and the blocked fraction φ = x·V_A/V_fu.
- If the φ term's error bar includes zero, drop it. H_R then depends on Li content only, or not at all. Use a constant with a ±40% band.
- The lattice-gas paper says H_R depends on Li concentration. That makes Li content the likelier driver, not V_A.
- The plan also says how small a change the data can detect.
- I can't tell yet whether a fit will work. It depends on your real H_R values.

**Added to the plan after your vacancy point**
- A check of whether vacancies cluster near each dopant (S5), and an effective n_active if they do.
- A warning about double counting with y(r).
- A STOP rule: if the exponential closure test and the collapse test fail, don't run 5/16. Keep V_A and the radius as the result.
- The cubic-stability window drawn on the σ plot.
- Four open questions at the end. The first is the cell size. I assumed 128 formula units, so 1/16 means 8 Al. Please check this in your dump.
